COMPARISON OF THE GOVERNMENT TO THE FULL ROOM, IN ACCORDANCE WITH ARTICLE 203 OF THE REGULATION: - Urgent membership of the Vice-President of the Government and Minister for Economic Transition and the Democratic Challenge to the Full Chamber, requested by Mrs GAMARRA RUIZ-CLAVIJO and 85 others, to inform about the state of work, so far from the technical point of view as from the negotiation with the European Commission and the entry-in-force period, of the Iberian proposal to fix a stop at the price of the gas and to support the ENERGY EXCEPTION OF THE ENERGY ISLAND OF THE IBERIAN PENINSULA. [[File number 210/000097]]
ParlaMint-ES_2022-06-30-CD220630.u200	We continue with the last item on the agenda, concerning the appearance of the third Vice-President of the Government and Minister for the Ecological Transition and the Demographic Challenge before the plenary of the House, requested by Mrs Gamarra Ruiz-Clavijo and another eighty-five Members, to report on the status of the work, both from the technical point of view and from the negotiation with the European Commission and the deadline for the entry into force of the Iberian proposal, to set a ceiling on the price of gas and to consolidate the energy exception or energy island of the Iberian peninsula. I give the floor to the third Vice-President of the Government and Minister for the Ecological Transition and the Demographic Challenge.
ParlaMint-ES_2022-06-30-CD220630.u201	Thank you very much, President. Thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen, for this invitation to appear. I am addressing this House today to share with you information on the implementation of the so-called Iberian mechanism and its results, as well as to report on the measures taken by the Government in the field of energy with a view to minimizing the economic consequences of the current crisis and equitably sharing the economic impacts of the Russian war on energy. As you know, for the past year we have been experiencing a sharp increase in the price of electricity in the wholesale market, which has an important impact on the rest of the economy. It is a price increase that is not due either to changes in supply or to changes in demand, or even to changes in the relative cost of most of the technologies used to generate electricity; it is solely due to the contagion effect of the gas price increase on the price of electricity as a result of Europe's regulatory design in this area. Therefore, from the very beginning - for many months now, in fact a year ago - Spain has been pointing out that the magnitude of this crisis required urgent, joint and coordinated action by the countries of the European Union. It is an approach that, months later, was endorsed by European leaders at the European Council on 24 and 25 March. We have worked on a proposal that has finally been recognised by what is commonly called the Iberian exception in the colloquial nature, justified in the very low interconnection of the Peninsula with the rest of the continent, which limits competition on the market and hinders the plurality of prices for consumers. It is an interconnection close to 2.8%, which prevents us from enjoying the advantages and benefits of the internal electricity market in an equivalent way as the rest of the Europeans do, with the exception of Cyprus. In reality, there is practically no other good or service occurring in any Member State of the European Union which has a physical limitation of these characteristics in order to participate in the benefits of the internal market. Consequently, the possibility of adopting a temporary measure of 12 months' duration, which limits the impact that the rise in natural gas prices is having on the wholesale electricity market in Spain and Portugal, was supported. I will summarise very quickly what the measure consists of. Essentially, it consists in the introduction of a limit to the price at which gas plants are offered for electricity generation. After the sale on the market, the system prorates the cost of the adjustment to compensate for the actual cost of gas among the benefitted consumers and the most immediate effect is that the only electricity paid at the cost of gas is electricity generated by gas. Let us, therefore, immediately mitigate the undesirable contagion effect on the rest of the technologies and reduce in a very important way what is known as the fallen benefits of the sky for the rest of the technologies. The mechanism, after weeks of rigorous work and negotiation with Portugal and the Commission, came into force on 15 June and let me tell you that it did so under particularly adverse conditions: with an intense heat wave, with unorbited gas prices following Gazprom's 40 % gas supply announcement to Europe and a major incident on the main liquefactory plant on the west coast of the United States, from where most of the liquefied natural gas comes out to Europe, and with a very low generation of our renewable plants as a result of the lack of wind resources in full anti-cyclone and the impact of heat on the performance of photovoltaic energy. However, despite these adverse conditions, the wholesale market prices and the final prices after adjustment were those days below those we would have had in the absence of the mechanism and below those of comparable European Union partners. The average price of the daily market, together with the cost of the adjustment, compared with the price during these first days of application, is above 14 % below the price we would have had in the absence of the measure. In fact, if we compare ourselves during these days with other countries, we will see that the first half of June, from 1 to 14, the price in the wholesale market in Spain remained in the middle of the average of countries such as France, Italy or Germany. However, from the 15th of June everything changes. We have gone from having similar prices in Spain, with and without the adjustment cost, to having significantly lower prices day by day to day to the rest of the Member States, to the point where during the second half of June, on average, with the application of the mechanism, the price on the wholesale market without adjustment has been between 45 and 55 % lower than the price in Germany, France or Italy. I would say that the Iberian exception, even in the first two weeks, has proved to be effective in at least reducing wholesale market prices and the extraordinary benefits of companies, protecting consumers, reducing the electricity bill and enabling a practical test that can be applied in the rest of Europe in situations of stress such as the current ones to decouple the electricity market from high gas prices. These are positive results. Today prices in Europe have also been much higher than ours: in France, 362 euros per megawatt hour; in Italy, 364; in Germany, 323. In Spain, adding to the wholesale price the cost of adjustment, 193. That is to say, Spain today obtains price differences in the environment of 130 to 170 euros the megawatt hour with the majority of European countries in the wholesale market and a very important difference after applying the adjustment. These results automatically translate into consumer protection. In just 15 days, the mechanism has managed to reduce the extraordinary benefits of electricity companies to the benefit of all consumers by around EUR 250 million. And, which is also very important, the effect is also transferred to the estimate of electricity prices for the coming quarters, resulting in very significant differences between countries. I give you a data: for the fourth quarter of the year the analysts predict that the price in Spain is 149 euros the megawatt hour; in Germany, 343 euros the megawatt hour, and in France, given the terrible situation of its nuclear park, 723 euros the megawatt hour. In other words, these future price estimates demonstrate, confirm the confidence of analysts in the proper functioning of the Iberian mechanism. But if we add to the implementation of the Iberian mechanism all the measures that the Government has been taking - the tax reduction, the reduction of charges, the reduction of the extraordinary profits associated with the price of gas and the invoices of households hosted at the PVPC rate - they are almost 30% cheaper than they would have been without them. On the other hand, I would like to stress that this mechanism can, as I said earlier, be a good starting point to inspire solutions at European level; in principle, to respond with extraordinary proposals to extraordinary situations, but also to open up a debate on how to develop price regulation in the wholesale market in a way that is effective and that makes the electrification we need attractive. It is true, ladies and gentlemen, that the energy context in which I appear before you is extraordinary, complex and - I would add - worrying too, is worrying in the short term. Therefore, having a committed, hard-working and solidarity-priority government is crucial; having a Parliament that supports this approach is also crucial. This is undoubtedly one of the most important issues of European policy at this time; indeed, my appearance coincides, at a particularly interesting time, with the satisfaction of being able to convey to all of you the progress that the European Union has made this week in reaching a political agreement in the Energy Council and in the Environment Council on the Fit For 55 package, the objectives of increasing ambition in reducing emissions and transforming our energy system for the whole continent, progress on the basis of a political agreement on the whole package. The objective was to reduce EU emissions by 55% by 2030. In particular, we have achieved a policy orientation on renewable energy and energy efficiency directives, so as to speed up the transition by raising the renewable energy target to 40% by 2030; extending the emissions trading regime to maritime and land transport and construction; setting zero emissions targets for new vehicles registered from 2035 onwards, or creating a new social climate fund, which will provide strong coverage for vulnerable households, SMEs and transport users in rural and remote areas without access to public transport, because a transformation like this is not just protesting, it is done above all by ensuring fairness in the measures we take, ensuring that the distributive impacts of their implementation do not generate more differences, but on the contrary. We also devote a very large part of the Energy Council meeting to discussing Europe's concerted and effective responses following the adoption of the sixth sanctions package to Russia and the imperative need to agree and strengthen national contingency plans, including, where appropriate, the need to strengthen savings and efficiency policies or even to raise the most appropriate responses to the hypothetical case where it is essential to prioritize the availability of energy resources among consumers. The situation is not easy. Let me remind you of some data. In recent weeks the flow of Russian gas through Nord Stream has been reduced by around 70 %, increasing the price of gas in the Dutch reference market from EUR 80 megawatt hour to EUR 130 megawatt hour, i.e. an increase of more than 60 % in a few weeks. On 8 June, the liquefaction plant on the east coast of the United States, the main port of origin of liquefied natural gas destined for Europe, suffered an incident that has left a drop in the export capacity of 60 million cubic meters per day for three weeks. These are tensions that, despite Europe's coordinated response, can continue to increase, are tensions that have to cause us to reflect on the need to be prepared, as indicated by the International Energy Agency or the European Commission, for the alleged total cut in Russian gas supply, a threat of cuts that has materialized with cuts in supply to Poland, Bulgaria, Finland, the Netherlands, Denmark..., following the refusal of its operators to accept the payment system imposed by Putin in rubles of the supply contracts signed in order not to violate the sanctions established by the European Union. This risk explains why eleven European Union countries have already activated an early warning level as a result of a potential Russian gas cut; explains why Germany, for the first time in its history and given the possibility that Russia could cut gas supply from one day to the next, has activated the state of alert prior to the emergency situation envisaged by the current European Union Supply Safety Regulation; explains why the three delegated advisors of the three largest French energy companies have publicly asked all citizens to maximize their energy savings efforts from now on. In this scenario, Spain, although better prepared, cannot neglect either its domestic response or its European commitment, so we work to ensure that this hypothesis never materializes. But we know that we must also promote measures of diversification of suppliers, of impulse to renewables and biogas, policies of savings and efficiency, electrification, deployment of small installations, sum of measures that can become decisive in the coming autumn, in the coming winter. In the contingency plans of all Member States it will be essential to prioritise savings and to establish the criteria to be applied in the hypothetical event that the situation becomes more complicated. At the outset, in our country we have wanted to promote savings measures in the field of the General Administration of the State that strengthen public transport, remote work, measures of efficiency in the air conditioning and lighting of buildings and outdoors or self-consumption, and in the coming weeks the European Commission will present specific recommendations on this for all the Member States of the European Union. There is no doubt that Europe has opted for the change of energy model and the fight against climate change as essential policies for its security, for economic modernisation and for social construction and for the European project, clearly reinforced reasons in view of the virulence of the impact that recent developments in international commodity markets are having on energy prices. Let us think that in one year the price of gas has multiplied by six, that of the barrel of oil has increased by 74% and that of coal has tripled, an increase in prices involving various factors: the growing mismatch between supply and demand as a result of investment in new energy less than we need, a fall in investment in fossil fuel farms, an increase in the overall demand for energy higher than expected as a result of economic reactivations at the end of the pandemic, factors which are inexhaustibly and dramatically compounded by the instability caused by Russia since the summer of 2021 with regard to gas supplies to Europe and the tension resulting from the invasion of Ukraine, an aggressive strategy by Russia, qualified by many as an energy blackmail, which seeks to weaken the domestic economies and the European productive fabric, to double the rejection of civil society to this war or to reduce the margin of manoeuvre of the European institutions and governments of the Member States in adopting measures and sanctions to resolve this matter as soon as possible. It is an intentional and articulated action around the management of natural gas storage in Gazprom, in historical minimums for a year, 70% of the usual, as confirmed by the Bruegel Institute. The reduction or interruption of the flow of natural gas to Europe since September 2021 shows that, although we have opened decades, we use ancient war techniques, with a different face. But the very high European dependence on Russian energy raw materials becomes an element of risk for destabilisation, which has effects beyond the European continent. That is why Europe's united, solidarity-based, flexible and coherent response is fundamental, fundamental in Europe and would also be fundamental in each of the European Member States. What is at stake are the principles and values on which the European integration project is founded: respect for national sovereignty, fundamental rights and individual freedoms, universal access to basic necessities as energy products, including food, the strong European commitment to combating climate change, which implies the progressive abandonment of fossil fuels so as not to incur more suffering, more costs, more inequality, more catastrophe, and our responsibility to third parties as protagonists in building relations of peace and prosperity. [[The Lord]] These are principles that are not negotiated, that are not doubled. The response given by society and the European institutions has therefore focused on the establishment of trade and financial sanctions on the Putin regime and on the rapid reduction of our dependence on Russian fuels. Ultimately, this crisis has highlighted the vulnerability and the climate, social, economic and strategic risks associated with an energy model based on fossil fuels in terms of security of supply, autonomy, stability and affordability of energy prices. That is why, from the outset, we have understood that it is essential to promote united European action, a coordinated response at European level, in order to tackle this situation. And this has been the approach with which the Government has been working from the outset with initiatives and proposals that we are proud of and which today are widely reflected in the measures proposed by the Commission and which are endorsed by the vast majority of Member States. These are proposals and responses that have made it possible to strengthen close coordination between Member States and with third countries, necessary to address this crisis. They have made it possible to drastically reduce Russia's dependence in the very short term and on fossil fuels on the strategic horizon, which we need to achieve as soon as possible. Therefore, more than ever, in these circumstances, I ask you for a sense of state; a sense of state to protect vulnerable consumers, middle classes and productive sectors; a sense of state to share efforts equitably; a sense of state to anticipate and accelerate the energy transition by adequately addressing the root causes and using our efforts and resources from the recovery plan in the early deployment of a new clean, efficient, price-stable, predictable and affordable energy system. If the energy transition for climate reasons was urgent before, today it is even more urgent for economic and social justice reasons. Fortunately, in Spain we have encountered a good part of the advanced duties. We had worked on the diagnosis, the identification of opportunities, the identification of difficulties, the construction of an agenda, the promotion of industrial and social capacities. Our response is clear and committed: in the short term, ensuring effective and immediate measures to alleviate the impacts of energy prices and strengthen existing supply security thanks to the great diversity of suppliers, our infrastructures and the need to increase the momentum of electrification, efficiency and savings; in the medium term, accelerating the implementation of structural and shutdown measures that allow us to promote the agility of the transition by generalizing renewable solutions already known in the solar and wind fields on different scales, with the maximum participation of citizens and small developers, promoting savings generated by changes in our homes, in mobility and in industrial processes and, at the same time, facilitating the anticipated deployment of the most innovative solutions that will be key in the coming years, such as the storage of electricity, innovative renewables, such as offshore wind or floating photovoltaics, or renewable hydrogen. It is a deployment that requires regulatory frameworks and own or strengthened infrastructure to respond on the basis of a stable and permanent commitment by a government that takes all the measures it needs in the domestic sphere and contributes responsibly to shaping the most appropriate responses at European level. And let me insist on both dimensions. We do not live in a bubble, we have to work at national level, we have to work at European level; only by activating the two do we achieve an effective response to one of the biggest global energy crises. In Spain, fortunately, the exposure to Russia is smaller. The strength of our infrastructure, diversification of suppliers and indigenous response capacity are very high, but it does not make us immune, we are still exposed to price increases. That is why the responses adopted and the Government's commitment to a just social strategy to deal with these difficult times is fundamental. We have been putting this forward since June 2021 with successive packages of measures to impose an 80% reduction in the tax burden on electricity consumers, to be added the reduction in the regulated costs of the bill, charges, which are the responsibility of this government, up to 56% compared to those in force a year ago, when the price crisis began in June 2021. And acting on the two components of the bill in which we could do so, taxation and fixed costs, we have exceptionally added the extraordinary reduction in tolls on the transport and distribution of electricity for the industry. To this must be added the top of the gas of the Iberian mechanism - which I have already shared - but also the reduction of the extraordinary profits that the electricity companies came to obtain from the consumers in the absence of the instrument that we approved in Decree Law 17/2021, of urgent measures to mitigate the impact of the price escalation of natural gas in the retail markets of gas and electricity, which allows us to recover up to 90% of the extraordinary profits generated in contracted energy at a price more than 67 euros megawatt hour. In September we adopted this measure, which is an exceptional instrument now recognised in the Commission's communications of 8 March and 18 May inviting Member States to take measures to reverse these extraordinary benefits, either through regulation, as Spain had done, or through taxation. A minority, which some described recklessly and frivolously as terrorist interventionism, becomes a fundamental reference in a very large part of the countries in our environment. Its application has a double effect: on the one hand, it detracts a large part of the extraordinary benefits that can be generated - more than 340 million euros in our case - but also, and above all, it has served to limit the rise in tariffs at the time when it was renewed. It has been customary for traders not to update these prices above EUR 67 per megawatt hour in order to prevent precisely the activation of the reduction, i.e. it has been an effective measure to protect consumers. Measures to contain the impact of the price crisis do not focus exclusively on necessity, they also extend to the field of natural gas and fuels. We have limited the rise of the regulated gas bill to 5% per quarter. We have guaranteed the bonus for fuels of 20 cents per litre, applying since March. From the very beginning, this government has acted to promote a fair distribution of costs and efforts, primarily protecting the most vulnerable and middle classes, so we have increased the discount on the electric social bonus by 60% for vulnerable groups and 70% for severe vulnerable groups. We have created the figure of the minimum vital supply, which protects vulnerable consumers from cuts, and we have increased the amount of the thermal social bonus and included the recipients of the minimum vital income. They are protective measures which have also been adopted to preserve the economic competitiveness of the industries most exposed to energy prices and which are the basis of our productive fabric. As I said before, we have reduced 80 % of tolls for electric intensive consumers, we have increased compensation for indirect CO2 emissions costs, we have facilitated the flexibility of gas contracts so that industrial consumers can adjust their contracts to consumer needs, and we have promoted direct aid within the temporary framework of aid for large gas consumers. And now I'm going to ask you a question, because you're very entertaining. Do you know how many times the right wing of this country has supported any of these measures in Parliament? I'll make it clear to you, none. None! Their mouths are filled protesting, but they have never supported a single measure; on the contrary, it gives the impression that they are wanting everything to go wrong. From here I appeal to the memory of the people, because this is not the first time that they apply that of 'the sinking Spain, that we will raise it'. But this time they won't get it. [[Applauds]] Last Saturday, the Council of Ministers extended many of these protection measures until the end of the year, in addition to being reinforced by complementary measures, such as the 5% reduction in VAT for electricity, placing us as the European Union country with the lowest VAT, together with Malta; the maintenance of the existing rebates for the social bonus; the freezing of the maximum price of butane candy until the end of the year at EUR 19.55; the approval of a EUR 200 aid for low incomes, and a transitional increase for six months of 15% of minimum vital income payrolls and non-contributory retirement and invalidity pensions, and the extension of the bonus to fuels of 20 cents per litre until the end of the year, still being the fastest and most effective way to protect consumers from increases in fuel prices. To all this, we have accompanied measures to support public transport, thanks to the reduction of 50% of the price of all monthly allowances and land travel titles of state competition and the reduction of more than 30% of transport allowances of autonomous communities and local entities, to which we hope that the autonomous communities will be added in order to be able to materialise this reduction as soon as possible; above all, we hope that they will be able to make their contribution in order to be able to achieve a reduction of 50% in this case. In addition, we maintain and extend direct aid to the intensive gas industries and are designing a new tax that will tax the extraordinary profits of energy companies. In short, with the measures adopted three months ago and with the new package of measures adopted on Saturday, we believe that the CPI will be contained in 3.5 points, avoiding a rise in inflation which, in its absence, would be much greater. We know that this is not enough, we know that what has been described so far are important, capital measures, on which we must always pay attention, but they are emergency measures, the calls to contain the dramatic situation in which we are in. Above all, we need substantive measures, capable of addressing the causes of the problem or even preparing for a deterioration of the situation in the event that this becomes worse. That is why, in line with what was agreed in Europe on 18 May, we have strengthened cooperation between gas and energy operators, taking decisions that require a cautious storage policy also in Spain. Our dynamic winter plan has been modified to strengthen safety in the period of increased consumption and Decree Law 6/2022 established that natural gas warehouses must be at 90% of their capacity on October 31st in order to be able to cope with the winter more calmly. This is an objective incorporated in advance in March, well before its adoption last week by the European Parliament, which determined the need to have 80 % of the covered storage capacity by the end of 2022 and 90 % by 2023. At present the underground warehouses in Spain are 72 % of their capacity, and the storage of LNG, at around 80 %, is extremely positive compared to those of other States, such as France, 57; Italy, 55; or Germany, 42. Along with immediate response measures, I would like to remind you of some of the structural response, which guarantees us in the medium and long term a drastic reduction in our dependence on fossil fuels. The production, transport and intelligent consumption of energy are essential, promoting digitisation, saving and efficiency; electrification of end-uses wherever possible is essential, and the development and deployment of renewables, self-consumption and new energy technologies and energy vectors such as biogas or renewable hydrogen or solutions such as storage are essential. They are three major axes that involve innovation, industry, employment, improving quality of life and benefits and adequate responses to the situation we suffer. They involve measures to accelerate the energy transition, aligned with the European energy and climate project, and a key orientation reinforced with the resources of the Next Generation funds in a strategic and efficient way to allow the deployment of self-consumption, through the release of 10% of the capacity of the nodes of the distribution transport networks, which explains why since June 2018 the self-consumption has multiplied by 5.5 times in our country; that they require the regulation of floating photovoltaics and offshore wind; that they require the replenishment of the pioneering wind installations that are coming to the end of their useful life; that they require us to promote the use of renewable gases, such as biogas or renewable hydrogen, with the aim of facilitating the decarbonisation of industry and end-uses of energy, in accordance with the principles of circular economy and the exploitation of indigenous resources, and that they require the deployment of electric mobility, including the charging infrastructure. We are working on the development of a flexible system, adapted to a renewable high penetration horizon; a system that requires proper regulation of capacity markets, smart networks, digitisation, demand management. Therefore, being in the context of regulatory sandboxes, allows us, in a controlled and narrowed environment, to test regulatory improvements and new ones, as well as to deploy the regulation of new figures to then be able to transfer them with certainty and certainty to the general regulatory framework. The data confirms that we are going in the right direction, managing to channel our renewable potential with the investor appetite. In 2018 there were 23.4 gigawatts of wind and 4.7 of photovoltaics; in 2022, there were 29.3 wind and 16.2 of photovoltaics, a growth of 25% and 344 % respectively. Ladies and gentlemen, as you see, at national level we have acted at all possible levels, and we continue to do so, but the response to this crisis - you have heard me say it already - necessarily passes through the European level. The proposals put forward by Spain reiterate the need to promote energy infrastructure through the development of electricity interconnections on the peninsula and the rest of the continent, responding in solidarity to the European request that Spain provide coverage to other countries of the Union, also in the field of gas, making clear the importance of not taking steps back and working for a gas infrastructure that is prepared for hydrogen or promoting the creation of a joint gas procurement platform, which, like the purchase of masks during the pandemic, would strengthen the negotiating power of the European Union on international markets. Or, finally, leading the proposal to open a rigorous and in-depth debate on the current rules of the electricity market, echoed last week by President Ursula von der Leyen, compromising the presentation of a proposal for the regulation of the electricity market, whose current rules of operation have not prevented the contagion of gas prices on electricity and involve a very important drain of economic resources for European industry and families. In Spain, the Government understood that these circumstances required the adoption of extraordinary firewall measures, and proposed to its partners to initiate a quiet debate, open to all actors, to reform market rules, both temporarily, in the face of extraordinary adverse circumstances, and structurally, facilitating the evolution towards the needs and characteristics of our energy systems in the twenty-first century. It will be a debate that begins in October with the proposal for reform promised by the President of the Commission. The trial of what happens here will help us to know better how to do it. It will be an important sign, since wholesale prices, their impact on future prices, allow us to work on different fronts; in our case, in the Spanish case, also to work on another major pending reform: to reduce the volatility of the regulated tariff. The high volatility of the market highlights the need to reformulate the tariff to find a balance between cost competitiveness and greater stability so that domestic consumers and SMEs can better plan their energy expenditure; it is also the rate at which vulnerable consumers, who receive the social bonus, are welcomed, making it particularly necessary and essential to undertake the reform that stabilizes their bills. That, without the Iberian mechanism, without signs of lower prices of present and future, would be a clumsy undertaking a reform for which price references have to be existing references, not references of the past. Therefore, once the Iberian mechanism is in place, we have committed ourselves to making the necessary changes to the methodology for calculating the PVPC tariff before October this year in order to introduce a more stable price reference, such as the futures of electricity. Our forecast is that the new energy costing formula for the regulated tariff can start to apply in early 2023. The impact on future prices and prices also facilitates an expansion of the supply of products allowing medium- and long-term procurement between developers, traders, industrialists and large consumers. In this area, measures to reduce risks are particularly important. Today we have domestic instruments such as the CESCE FERGIE fund, created by this government, but we also propose that instruments developed for the whole of Europe by the European Investment Bank. There are many outstanding issues, both in the short term, consolidating an equitable cost-benefit response, including the adoption of the specific tax on energy companies, and in the medium and long term, accelerating structural change. A horizon that must inspire the revision of the integrated energy and climate plan, adapting it to this new context and including the mobilizing effect of both the Recovery, Transformation and Resilience Plan and the need to advance objectives in this context. The recovery plan represents a huge opportunity for Spain not only to develop and accelerate renewable deployment, but also to be able to generate the transformation of our economy that drives the entire associated value chain. Ladies and gentlemen, the circumstances we face are extremely turbulent. These are turbulences, conflicts, disruptive difficulties that can occur at any time and anywhere. ParlaMint-ES_2022-06-30-CD220630.u216	Thank you very much. For the Confederal Parliamentary Group of the United Nations Podemos-En Communo Podemos - Galicia in Communo, which has its turn in this turn, Mr Echenique Robba has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2022-06-30-CD220630.u217	Thank you, President. He has devoted more time to insects and the underwater volcano than to the gas top. I'm hallucinating with the extreme right, which, by the way, is being put on the face of Citizens lately. Ladies and gentlemen, Vice-President-third, it is a challenge for me to intervene in a matter as important as this for the working people and small businesses of our country, that of the bill of light. As it is also a complex issue, I will try to be as pedagogical as possible, thus facilitating parliamentary debate and political debate. First of all, how does the electricity market work? Except for long-term contracts and new renewable auctions, the electricity market operates under a mechanism called a marginalist. What is this about? First, we estimate the electricity demand that is going to be in the country as a whole in the morning for timetables. Secondly, the different power stations offer energy, usually measured in megawatt hours at the price they freely choose. Then, the lowest-priced bids are accepted, i.e. starting with the cheapest offers, and so on until the demand of the whole country is met. According to this design, obviously the last offer that is accepted is the most expensive one, which has a higher price per megawatt hour. And this is where the logic, so called, marginalist appears. Instead of paying each offer at the price offered, which would be logical, all megawatt hours are paid at the price of the last offer, i.e. the most expensive, regardless of the cost of production of each plant. Let us set a concrete example so that we can see the dimension of the scam. Yesterday, 29 June, according to data from the Ministry of Ecological Transition, if the gas cap had not been activated, the average price of electricity in the wholesale market would have been 271 euros megawatt hour. This would have been the average price of the last bids to enter the auction, the price of the most expensive bid and the price at which all bids would be paid: 271 euros. Now let us bear in mind that producing a megawatt of nuclear time, for example, costs around 40 euros. Therefore, we would be paying it at almost seven times its cost and the benefit would be almost 800 %. In turn, producing a megawatt of hydropower costs around 10 euros. In this case, we would be paying it at twenty-seven times its cost and the profit would be 2600 %. No wonder, therefore, that this is called the fallen benefits of heaven, although, of course, stealing would be a much more precise word. Yeah, steal. Since I can't be bought with a revolving door, I can say: steal. Having said that, how does the gas cap work? How does the Iberian exception work? Well, in addition to nuclear power, hydroelectric power and all the others, there is one type of power plant that runs on gas. Like right now, due largely to the invasion of Ukraine, gas is very expensive in international markets, these gas plants are very hard to buy raw materials and therefore have to sell their very expensive megawatt hour. As the marginalist market allocates to all technologies the most expensive price, without the top that the government has put on we would all be paying at gas prices. Therefore, what the Iberian mechanism does is to put a stop to the price of the raw material and this is transferred to an effective stop in the price of the megawatt hour of the gas plants, which typically makes the price of the valuation, that is, the price of the last offer, lower than if the stop did not exist. Finally, because gas stations cannot work at losses, they are compensated for the money they have stopped collecting. In this way we have altered the marginalist system, because we are paying gas at gas prices, but the other technologies at a significantly cheaper price. In other words, we cannot prevent gas from being extremely expensive in international markets, but we can reduce the sky's falling profits from power plants that do not need gas to produce energy and thus lower the bill for light. And this that sounds so good in theory, does it work in practice? The answer is yes. Let's take the example of yesterday again. As I said, if the gas cap were not active, the average price would have been about 271 euros per megawatt hour. Thanks to the Iberian mechanism, the average valuation price was about 133 euros yesterday, about half. But let us not forget, as the spokesman for the PP said very well, that compensation must be added to the gas plants; yesterday this amounted to about 86 euros, with which the final price was about 219 euros, a reduction of 20% regarding what we would have paid without limit, although the spokesman of the People's Party does not like. Indeed, the mechanism works. If we compare it with the countries in our environment, we come to the same conclusion. Yesterday we paid around 30% less than in Germany and around 35% less than in France or Italy, and yesterday was not an exceptional day, but rather a more or less typical day. So, congratulations, third vice president, and congratulations to the entire coalition government. We are talking about a courageous and, moreover, effective measure. By the way, when União Podemos proposed already many months ago to put a stop to the marginalist price, which is basically the same thing that this mechanism does, we were told of everything less beautiful. We are very happy that it has finally been shown that it could be done, because that means that we are going to relieve the bill of many people in Spain. By the way, and this is where you launched the first proposal from our parliamentary group, Mrs Rivera, you are sensitively down from the light is only automatic in what we call PVPC or regulated market. But there is also the free market in Spain, which is, if I may, the second part of the scam. It's very easy to explain. In the free market the price of energy is not defined as I have commented, but it is agreed between the company and the customer. If we take into account that the big electrics are not sisters of charity, if they can offer the price that gives them the will always will get a profit, and, in fact, historically the prices of the free market have been almost always higher than those of the regulated market. That's why Mr. Sánchez Galán, allegedly hiring Mafia Villarejo to extort his business rivals, the other day called his own customers who are in the regulated market fools, and he did it because he wanted them to move on to the free market in order to cheat is better. Since you can't buy me with a revolving door, I can also say this clearly. [[Applauds]] The reduction of the bill due to the gas cap will only apply automatically in the regulated market, and that is why my group proposes that all kinds of permanence or sanctions be eliminated in the free market contracts so that people can freely do the opposite of what Mr Sánchez Galán has said and can be passed on to the regulated market. The second proposal we want to make to you, Madam Third Vice-President, has to do with the government's agreement. To decarbonize the economy, to curb climate change, we cannot reduce the energy transition to a process of replacing the technology with which electricity is produced. If that is the only approach, the effects can already be felt by the 18 million people who currently pay for electricity receipts: the formation of an oligopoly that, because of its high profitability, attracts investment funds and controls the entire sector, disappears competition and imposes speculative and arbitrary prices on consumers. The European Union and international organisations have pointed out that it is an essential feature of the energy transition to change this centralized model of large enterprises, which build large power plants that colonize the rural environment, which concentrate on the market and production; a change to a decentralized model in which citizens assume a role in what has been called prosumers, that is, consumers who also produce. Surprisingly, this perspective is almost excluded from the legislation and energy policy that is being discussed in Parliament. In order to reduce the price of electricity in a structural and not only cyclical way, we must bet, without doubt, on the distributed generation and adopting the legal reforms that are necessary in the Electricity Sector Act and in the decrees that develop the National Integrated Energy and Climate Plan. We have therefore tabled dozens of amendments in favour of the distributed generation, in favour of self-consumption, to achieve management and control over the deployment of renewables in the draft law on self-consumption, but curiously the parliamentary process is stopped. The same applies to the amendments to the draft law deriving from Decree 6/2022, so that acceleration does not prevent environmental assessment with public participation, with amendments to democratize access to energy and strengthen in Spain the figure of the prosumer, which would make the energy transition real, such as amendments not to apply forced expropriation in the deployment of renewables dominated by large companies. We have also proposed in these amendments the boost of storage through a figure which is the storage of municipal control electricity, so that municipal storage organized neighborhood to neighborhood boosts the role of citizens' prosumer. But all this parliamentary discussion is stalled. Moreover, we propose another more immediate step for decrees 6 and 11 of 2022. Review in depth the environmental zoning map for the deployment of renewable energies published by MITECO on 9 May, which contains some errors in the types of protection - errors that are only detected or observed when a more detailed scale is used - and builds an environmental zoning map that includes a classification of suitable areas to accommodate these renewable facilities. I mean, let's turn it around, let's say where you can and not where there are different degrees of condition, if the condition is low, medium, high or very high. That map should be well done and rigorous in its decisions. To be rigorous, this zoning of suitability is a planning that obviously requires a prior, rigorous and transparent strategic environmental assessment. From this premise, and with the impetus of the distributed generation, the energy transition could already be accelerated to the goal that we had set ourselves as a country. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES_2022-06-30-CD220630.u281	One of the things we found and decided not to change because it seemed to us that the historical one showed that it was advantageous was the regulated tariff, known as the PVPC, which the former government established; a regulated tariff that has entailed in the accumulated one enormous advantages, since there was no margin of increase, was directly linked to the price of the wholesale market. And, indeed, we differ from the rest of the countries of the European Union; there is no Member State of the European Union in which there is such a high percentage of consumers who have a tariff of these characteristics.
